Hi, doctor. Well... I'm a building cleaner, and I'm here because... well, it's the rats. Or, just the thought of seeing a rat, really. It's gotten so bad that I can't even do parts of my job anymore, like going into the basement or the trash rooms. I mean, I know in my head it's probably just an old building and it's not really dangerous, but I just can't do it.

I always tell my sister, Mary—she works at a bakery down the street, makes the absolute best sourdough—I tell her they just don't make floors like that anymore. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, panic is exactly the word for it. If I even just hear a little scratching sound near a wall, or if I see a shadow move by one of those old pipes, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake so bad, and I just feel like I have to get out of there before I can even think straight. 

I actually dropped my whole keyring last week because my hands were shaking so much. Which is a nightmare, because there are like forty keys on that ring. My supervisor was supposed to switch us to those electronic keycards months ago. They have them in the building next door—my friend Sarah works over there, and she just swipes her badge and the door opens. So much easier. She's got arthritis in her hands, you know, so turning those heavy deadbolts was getting really hard for her. Her doctor told her to try eating more turmeric for the inflammation, but I don't know if that actually works. Do you think spices actually do anything for joints? Because my knees have been acting up lately when I mop the main hall, and I was thinking about trying it... but I don't really like the taste of curry, so I'm not sure how I'd cook with it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's really just the rats. Or mice, I guess, anything like that. Spiders don't bother me, heights are fine... it's just the rodents. I mean, my neighbor has a pet snake and I don't mind that at all. Actually, he was telling me the other day that snakes make great pets because they're so quiet. Not like dogs. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. The judge was wearing this really bright yellow hat, though, so maybe that's what set him off. I read somewhere that dogs can't see all colors, but maybe yellow is one of the ones they can see? I don't know. But yeah, no, it's just the rats that make me feel that way.

Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. 

Speaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. 

Speaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... 

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.

No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. 

Actually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).\n  manifestation: I’m here because the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a trash room and not really dangerous.\n- key: S063\n  name: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\n  description: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: If I hear scratching near a wall or see something move by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away—my heart pounds, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight.\n- key: S064\n  name: Phobic_Avoidance_Endure\n  description: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: I turn down routes with basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or alley dumpsters when I can, and if I’m forced to clean one I keep the door open, rush through it, and feel scared the whole time.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve had to refuse or delay cleaning assignments in certain buildings, and my supervisor has started questioning whether I can be counted on for regular routes. I’m worried this will cost me better shifts or a chance at a lead position.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 0 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two years, since I moved into the older apartment building where people kept talking about rats.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S062\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you fear rats or mice when you're not at work, like when you're at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. \n\nSpeaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding rats the main reason you're avoiding certain areas, like the basement or trash rooms, at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. \n\nSpeaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? They chew through walls and pipes and... oh, speaking of pipes, the plumbing in my sister's house is an absolute nightmare. She lives over in the next county, in this old Victorian house she bought for a steal. It's beautiful, all that original woodwork, but the pipes are from, like, the 1920s. 

She called a plumber last week—his name was Frank, I think. Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.

If I even just hear a little scratching sound near a wall, or if I see a shadow move by one of those old pipes, my whole body reacts right away. Which is a nightmare, because there are like forty keys on that ring. My supervisor was supposed to switch us to those electronic keycards months ago. She's got arthritis in her hands, you know, so turning those heavy deadbolts was getting really hard for her. Her doctor told her to try eating more turmeric for the inflammation, but I don't know if that actually works. Do you think spices actually do anything for joints? Because my knees have been acting up lately when I mop the main hall, and I was thinking about trying it... but I don't really like the taste of curry, so I'm not sure how I'd cook with it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the thought of rats that causes you to panic, or are there other animals or situations that trigger a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats. Or mice, I guess, anything like that. Spiders don't bother me, heights are fine... it's just the rodents. I mean, my neighbor has a pet snake and I don't mind that at all. Actually, he was telling me the other day that snakes make great pets because they're so quiet. Not like dogs. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. The judge was wearing this really bright yellow hat, though, so maybe that's what set him off. I read somewhere that dogs can't see all colors, but maybe yellow is one of the ones they can see? I don't know. But yeah, no, it's just the rats that make me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear rats or mice when you're not at work, like when you're at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. \n\nSpeaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding rats the main reason you're avoiding certain areas, like the basement or trash rooms, at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. \n\nSpeaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? They chew through walls and pipes and... oh, speaking of pipes, the plumbing in my sister's house is an absolute nightmare. She lives over in the next county, in this old Victorian house she bought for a steal. It's beautiful, all that original woodwork, but the pipes are from, like, the 1920s. \n\nShe called a plumber last week—his name was Frank, I think. Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "determine if the fear is specific to the context of work or general", "question": "Does your fear of rats or mice affect your social life or relationships outside of work?"}

Well, I don't really know if it affects my social life, honestly. I mean, I don't go out all that much anyway. I usually just go to work and come home. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. She lives in this cute little duplex over by the park. 

It's a nice park, actually, they just put in new swings for the kids. The old ones were getting so rusty, I was always worried some poor child was going to get tetanus. Do they even give tetanus shots anymore? I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...

But, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well... I'm a building cleaner, and I'm here because... well, it's the rats. Or, just the thought of seeing a rat, really. It's gotten so bad that I can't even do parts of my job anymore, like going into the basement or the trash rooms. I mean, I know in my head it's probably just an old building and it's not really dangerous, but I just can't do it. \n\nSpeaking of the building, though, it's actually historic. They built it in the 1920s, I think? The lobby has this gorgeous original tile work. I always tell my sister, Mary—she works at a bakery down the street, makes the absolute best sourdough—I tell her they just don't make floors like that anymore. You have to use a special wax on them or it completely ruins the finish, and my supervisor never orders the right one, so I'm always having to buff it twice. It's exhausting. But anyway, what was I... oh, right. The trash rooms. I just can't go in them anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think about seeing a rat, do you ever feel like you're going to panic?\n\nPatient: Oh, panic is exactly the word for it. If I even just hear a little scratching sound near a wall, or if I see a shadow move by one of those old pipes, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake so bad, and I just feel like I have to get out of there before I can even think straight. \n\nI actually dropped my whole keyring last week because my hands were shaking so much. Which is a nightmare, because there are like forty keys on that ring. My supervisor was supposed to switch us to those electronic keycards months ago. They have them in the building next door—my friend Sarah works over there, and she just swipes her badge and the door opens. So much easier. She's got arthritis in her hands, you know, so turning those heavy deadbolts was getting really hard for her. Her doctor told her to try eating more turmeric for the inflammation, but I don't know if that actually works. Do you think spices actually do anything for joints? Because my knees have been acting up lately when I mop the main hall, and I was thinking about trying it... but I don't really like the taste of curry, so I'm not sure how I'd cook with it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the thought of rats that causes you to panic, or are there other animals or situations that trigger a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats. Or mice, I guess, anything like that. Spiders don't bother me, heights are fine... it's just the rodents. I mean, my neighbor has a pet snake and I don't mind that at all. Actually, he was telling me the other day that snakes make great pets because they're so quiet. Not like dogs. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. The judge was wearing this really bright yellow hat, though, so maybe that's what set him off. I read somewhere that dogs can't see all colors, but maybe yellow is one of the ones they can see? I don't know. But yeah, no, it's just the rats that make me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear rats or mice when you're not at work, like when you're at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. \n\nSpeaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding rats the main reason you're avoiding certain areas, like the basement or trash rooms, at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. \n\nSpeaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? They chew through walls and pipes and... oh, speaking of pipes, the plumbing in my sister's house is an absolute nightmare. She lives over in the next county, in this old Victorian house she bought for a steal. It's beautiful, all that original woodwork, but the pipes are from, like, the 1920s. \n\nShe called a plumber last week—his name was Frank, I think. Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats or mice affect your social life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if it affects my social life, honestly. I mean, I don't go out all that much anyway. I usually just go to work and come home. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. She lives in this cute little duplex over by the park. \n\nIt's a nice park, actually, they just put in new swings for the kids. The old ones were getting so rusty, I was always worried some poor child was going to get tetanus. Do they even give tetanus shots anymore? I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...\n\nBut, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...\n\nBut, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned earlier that it's really just the rats and mice that cause you to panic, but I want to clarify: would you say that your fear extends to other rodents, like squirrels or hamsters, as well?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's really just the rats and mice. I mean, squirrels are fine. They just stay up in the trees, mostly. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she actually feeds the squirrels in her backyard. She buys these huge bags of peanuts—in the shell, you know, the unsalted kind. She leaves them out on this little wooden picnic table she bought specifically for the squirrels. It's so tiny and cute. 

She got it at this craft fair they hold every autumn down by the river. They have all sorts of things there—handmade soaps, knitted scarves, those birdhouses painted to look like little cottages. I bought a jar of homemade apple butter there last year. It was delicious on toast, but I went back this year and the lady who makes it wasn't there. I asked around and someone said she moved to Florida to be closer to her grandkids. I guess a lot of people do that when they retire. Florida always seems so humid to me, though. My brother went to Orlando once for a conference and he said his glasses fogged up every time he stepped outside. He works in logistics, coordinating shipping routes or something like that. It sounds complicated, all those trucks and schedules...

But, uh, no, squirrels and hamsters don't bother me at all. It's just the rats and mice that make me panic.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well... I'm a building cleaner, and I'm here because... well, it's the rats. Or, just the thought of seeing a rat, really. It's gotten so bad that I can't even do parts of my job anymore, like going into the basement or the trash rooms. I mean, I know in my head it's probably just an old building and it's not really dangerous, but I just can't do it. \n\nSpeaking of the building, though, it's actually historic. They built it in the 1920s, I think? The lobby has this gorgeous original tile work. I always tell my sister, Mary—she works at a bakery down the street, makes the absolute best sourdough—I tell her they just don't make floors like that anymore. You have to use a special wax on them or it completely ruins the finish, and my supervisor never orders the right one, so I'm always having to buff it twice. It's exhausting. But anyway, what was I... oh, right. The trash rooms. I just can't go in them anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think about seeing a rat, do you ever feel like you're going to panic?\n\nPatient: Oh, panic is exactly the word for it. If I even just hear a little scratching sound near a wall, or if I see a shadow move by one of those old pipes, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake so bad, and I just feel like I have to get out of there before I can even think straight. \n\nI actually dropped my whole keyring last week because my hands were shaking so much. Which is a nightmare, because there are like forty keys on that ring. My supervisor was supposed to switch us to those electronic keycards months ago. They have them in the building next door—my friend Sarah works over there, and she just swipes her badge and the door opens. So much easier. She's got arthritis in her hands, you know, so turning those heavy deadbolts was getting really hard for her. Her doctor told her to try eating more turmeric for the inflammation, but I don't know if that actually works. Do you think spices actually do anything for joints? Because my knees have been acting up lately when I mop the main hall, and I was thinking about trying it... but I don't really like the taste of curry, so I'm not sure how I'd cook with it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the thought of rats that causes you to panic, or are there other animals or situations that trigger a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats. Or mice, I guess, anything like that. Spiders don't bother me, heights are fine... it's just the rodents. I mean, my neighbor has a pet snake and I don't mind that at all. Actually, he was telling me the other day that snakes make great pets because they're so quiet. Not like dogs. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. The judge was wearing this really bright yellow hat, though, so maybe that's what set him off. I read somewhere that dogs can't see all colors, but maybe yellow is one of the ones they can see? I don't know. But yeah, no, it's just the rats that make me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear rats or mice when you're not at work, like when you're at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. \n\nSpeaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding rats the main reason you're avoiding certain areas, like the basement or trash rooms, at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. \n\nSpeaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? They chew through walls and pipes and... oh, speaking of pipes, the plumbing in my sister's house is an absolute nightmare. She lives over in the next county, in this old Victorian house she bought for a steal. It's beautiful, all that original woodwork, but the pipes are from, like, the 1920s. \n\nShe called a plumber last week—his name was Frank, I think. Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats or mice affect your social life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if it affects my social life, honestly. I mean, I don't go out all that much anyway. I usually just go to work and come home. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. She lives in this cute little duplex over by the park. \n\nIt's a nice park, actually, they just put in new swings for the kids. The old ones were getting so rusty, I was always worried some poor child was going to get tetanus. Do they even give tetanus shots anymore? I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...\n\nBut, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that it's really just the rats and mice that cause you to panic, but I want to clarify: would you say that your fear extends to other rodents, like squirrels or hamsters, as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats and mice. I mean, squirrels are fine. They just stay up in the trees, mostly. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she actually feeds the squirrels in her backyard. I guess a lot of people do that when they retire. Florida always seems so humid to me, though. My brother went to Orlando once for a conference and he said his glasses fogged up every time he stepped outside. He works in logistics, coordinating shipping routes or something like that. It sounds complicated, all those trucks and schedules...\n\nBut, uh, no, squirrels and hamsters don't bother me at all. It's just the rats and mice that make me panic.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, I think I'd react the exact same way. It doesn't matter where it is. If I see one, or even if I just hear scratching near a wall or see something move by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away. My heart just starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight. 

It's like that fight or flight thing they talk about on those nature documentaries. I watch a lot of PBS. They have this one show on Sunday nights about the Serengeti. The lions are incredible, the way they coordinate their hunting. And the wildebeests, there are just thousands of them moving across the plains. It reminds me of when my husband and I took the kids to Yellowstone years ago. We saw a herd of bison crossing the road, and we just had to sit there in the minivan for twenty minutes while they took their sweet time. 

My husband, Dave, he kept trying to take pictures with this clunky old digital camera we had. It took a floppy disk, can you believe that? You could fit maybe ten pictures on one disk. Now everybody just uses their phones. The phones these days take better pictures than the professional cameras used to. My niece, Sarah, she takes all her wedding photography on her phone. She does these beautiful outdoor weddings. She had one last month at this vineyard up north. It rained, unfortunately, but they had these big white tents...

But, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the zoo. Yeah, no, even in a cage at a pet store, if I saw a mouse, I'd have that same panic. My heart would go crazy and I'd just need to run.

His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. The phones these days take better pictures than the professional cameras used to. My niece, Sarah, she takes all her wedding photography on her phone. She does these beautiful outdoor weddings. She had one last month at this vineyard up north. It rained, unfortunately, but they had these big white tents...\n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the zoo. Yeah, no, even in a cage at a pet store, if I saw a mouse, I'd have that same panic. My heart would go crazy and I'd just need to run.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould your reaction be any different if you saw a mouse compared to seeing a rat?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it wouldn't make a difference at all. A mouse, a rat... it's the exact same panic for me. Either one of them just sets me off completely. 

My sister, though, she thinks mice are actually cute. Can you imagine? She used to read those Beatrix Potter books to her kids—you know, the ones with the little mice in the bonnets and aprons? *The Tale of Two Bad Mice*, I think one was called. They had these gorgeous illustrations. My sister always loved watercolor paintings. She actually took a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago. She painted this lovely picture of a lighthouse for my mother's birthday. 

My mother loved lighthouses. She collected these little ceramic ones. She had a whole curio cabinet full of them in the dining room. Every time we went on vacation, we had to find a gift shop so she could buy a new one. We went to Cape Cod once when I was a teenager, and I swear we spent more time looking at ceramic lighthouses than we did at the actual ocean. The water there was freezing anyway. We had clam chowder in bread bowls, though, which was nice. I tried making clam chowder at home once, but it just didn't taste the same. I think I didn't use enough cream, or maybe it was the potatoes... I always buy the red potatoes, but maybe I should have used Russets. Russets are better for baking, I know that. I like a baked potato with just a little bit of butter and chives. My husband puts sour cream and bacon bits on his, but that's just too heavy for me, especially before bed.

But, um, yeah. A mouse or a rat, it doesn't matter. The size doesn't change anything for me. It's the same terrible feeling either way.
