[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well... I'm a building cleaner, and I'm here because... well, it's the rats. Or, just the thought of seeing a rat, really. It's gotten so bad that I can't even do parts of my job anymore, like going into the basement or the trash rooms. I mean, I know in my head it's probably just an old building and it's not really dangerous, but I just can't do it. \n\nSpeaking of the building, though, it's actually historic. They built it in the 1920s, I think? The lobby has this gorgeous original tile work. I always tell my sister, Mary—she works at a bakery down the street, makes the absolute best sourdough—I tell her they just don't make floors like that anymore. You have to use a special wax on them or it completely ruins the finish, and my supervisor never orders the right one, so I'm always having to buff it twice. It's exhausting. But anyway, what was I... oh, right. The trash rooms. I just can't go in them anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think about seeing a rat, do you ever feel like you're going to panic?\n\nPatient: Oh, panic is exactly the word for it. If I even just hear a little scratching sound near a wall, or if I see a shadow move by one of those old pipes, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake so bad, and I just feel like I have to get out of there before I can even think straight. \n\nI actually dropped my whole keyring last week because my hands were shaking so much. Which is a nightmare, because there are like forty keys on that ring. My supervisor was supposed to switch us to those electronic keycards months ago. They have them in the building next door—my friend Sarah works over there, and she just swipes her badge and the door opens. So much easier. She's got arthritis in her hands, you know, so turning those heavy deadbolts was getting really hard for her. Her doctor told her to try eating more turmeric for the inflammation, but I don't know if that actually works. Do you think spices actually do anything for joints? Because my knees have been acting up lately when I mop the main hall, and I was thinking about trying it... but I don't really like the taste of curry, so I'm not sure how I'd cook with it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the thought of rats that causes you to panic, or are there other animals or situations that trigger a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats. Or mice, I guess, anything like that. Spiders don't bother me, heights are fine... it's just the rodents. I mean, my neighbor has a pet snake and I don't mind that at all. Actually, he was telling me the other day that snakes make great pets because they're so quiet. Not like dogs. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. The judge was wearing this really bright yellow hat, though, so maybe that's what set him off. I read somewhere that dogs can't see all colors, but maybe yellow is one of the ones they can see? I don't know. But yeah, no, it's just the rats that make me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear rats or mice when you're not at work, like when you're at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. \n\nSpeaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding rats the main reason you're avoiding certain areas, like the basement or trash rooms, at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. \n\nSpeaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? They chew through walls and pipes and... oh, speaking of pipes, the plumbing in my sister's house is an absolute nightmare. She lives over in the next county, in this old Victorian house she bought for a steal. It's beautiful, all that original woodwork, but the pipes are from, like, the 1920s. \n\nShe called a plumber last week—his name was Frank, I think. Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats or mice affect your social life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if it affects my social life, honestly. I mean, I don't go out all that much anyway. I usually just go to work and come home. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. She lives in this cute little duplex over by the park. \n\nIt's a nice park, actually, they just put in new swings for the kids. The old ones were getting so rusty, I was always worried some poor child was going to get tetanus. Do they even give tetanus shots anymore? I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...\n\nBut, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that it's really just the rats and mice that cause you to panic, but I want to clarify: would you say that your fear extends to other rodents, like squirrels or hamsters, as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats and mice. I mean, squirrels are fine. They just stay up in the trees, mostly. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she actually feeds the squirrels in her backyard. She buys these huge bags of peanuts—in the shell, you know, the unsalted kind. She leaves them out on this little wooden picnic table she bought specifically for the squirrels. It's so tiny and cute. \n\nShe got it at this craft fair they hold every autumn down by the river. They have all sorts of things there—handmade soaps, knitted scarves, those birdhouses painted to look like little cottages. I bought a jar of homemade apple butter there last year. It was delicious on toast, but I went back this year and the lady who makes it wasn't there. I asked around and someone said she moved to Florida to be closer to her grandkids. I guess a lot of people do that when they retire. Florida always seems so humid to me, though. My brother went to Orlando once for a conference and he said his glasses fogged up every time he stepped outside. He works in logistics, coordinating shipping routes or something like that. It sounds complicated, all those trucks and schedules...\n\nBut, uh, no, squirrels and hamsters don't bother me at all. It's just the rats and mice that make me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would react the same way to seeing a rat or mouse in a completely different context, like at a zoo or in a pet store?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I think I'd react the exact same way. It doesn't matter where it is. If I see one, or even if I just hear scratching near a wall or see something move by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away. My heart just starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight. \n\nIt's like that fight or flight thing they talk about on those nature documentaries. I watch a lot of PBS. They have this one show on Sunday nights about the Serengeti. The lions are incredible, the way they coordinate their hunting. And the wildebeests, there are just thousands of them moving across the plains. It reminds me of when my husband and I took the kids to Yellowstone years ago. We saw a herd of bison crossing the road, and we just had to sit there in the minivan for twenty minutes while they took their sweet time. \n\nMy husband, Dave, he kept trying to take pictures with this clunky old digital camera we had. It took a floppy disk, can you believe that? You could fit maybe ten pictures on one disk. Now everybody just uses their phones. The phones these days take better pictures than the professional cameras used to. My niece, Sarah, she takes all her wedding photography on her phone. She does these beautiful outdoor weddings. She had one last month at this vineyard up north. It rained, unfortunately, but they had these big white tents...\n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the zoo. Yeah, no, even in a cage at a pet store, if I saw a mouse, I'd have that same panic. My heart would go crazy and I'd just need to run.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be any different if you saw a mouse compared to seeing a rat?\n\nPatient: No, it wouldn't make a difference at all. A mouse, a rat... it's the exact same panic for me. Either one of them just sets me off completely. \n\nMy sister, though, she thinks mice are actually cute. Can you imagine? She used to read those Beatrix Potter books to her kids—you know, the ones with the little mice in the bonnets and aprons? *The Tale of Two Bad Mice*, I think one was called. They had these gorgeous illustrations. My sister always loved watercolor paintings. She actually took a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago. She painted this lovely picture of a lighthouse for my mother's birthday. \n\nMy mother loved lighthouses. She collected these little ceramic ones. She had a whole curio cabinet full of them in the dining room. Every time we went on vacation, we had to find a gift shop so she could buy a new one. We went to Cape Cod once when I was a teenager, and I swear we spent more time looking at ceramic lighthouses than we did at the actual ocean. The water there was freezing anyway. We had clam chowder in bread bowls, though, which was nice. I tried making clam chowder at home once, but it just didn't taste the same. I think I didn't use enough cream, or maybe it was the potatoes... I always buy the red potatoes, but maybe I should have used Russets. Russets are better for baking, I know that. I like a baked potato with just a little bit of butter and chives. My husband puts sour cream and bacon bits on his, but that's just too heavy for me, especially before bed.\n\nBut, um, yeah. A mouse or a rat, it doesn't matter. The size doesn't change anything for me. It's the same terrible feeling either way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats or mice affect your social life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if it affects my social life, honestly. I mean, I don't go out all that much anyway. I usually just go to work and come home. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. She lives in this cute little duplex over by the park. \n\nIt's a nice park, actually, they just put in new swings for the kids. The old ones were getting so rusty, I was always worried some poor child was going to get tetanus. Do they even give tetanus shots anymore? I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...\n\nBut, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that it's really just the rats and mice that cause you to panic, but I want to clarify: would you say that your fear extends to other rodents, like squirrels or hamsters, as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats and mice. I mean, squirrels are fine. They just stay up in the trees, mostly. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she actually feeds the squirrels in her backyard. She buys these huge bags of peanuts—in the shell, you know, the unsalted kind. She leaves them out on this little wooden picnic table she bought specifically for the squirrels. It's so tiny and cute. \n\nShe got it at this craft fair they hold every autumn down by the river. They have all sorts of things there—handmade soaps, knitted scarves, those birdhouses painted to look like little cottages. I bought a jar of homemade apple butter there last year. It was delicious on toast, but I went back this year and the lady who makes it wasn't there. I asked around and someone said she moved to Florida to be closer to her grandkids. I guess a lot of people do that when they retire. Florida always seems so humid to me, though. My brother went to Orlando once for a conference and he said his glasses fogged up every time he stepped outside. He works in logistics, coordinating shipping routes or something like that. It sounds complicated, all those trucks and schedules...\n\nBut, uh, no, squirrels and hamsters don't bother me at all. It's just the rats and mice that make me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would react the same way to seeing a rat or mouse in a completely different context, like at a zoo or in a pet store?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I think I'd react the exact same way. It doesn't matter where it is. If I see one, or even if I just hear scratching near a wall or see something move by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away. My heart just starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight. \n\nIt's like that fight or flight thing they talk about on those nature documentaries. I watch a lot of PBS. They have this one show on Sunday nights about the Serengeti. The lions are incredible, the way they coordinate their hunting. And the wildebeests, there are just thousands of them moving across the plains. It reminds me of when my husband and I took the kids to Yellowstone years ago. We saw a herd of bison crossing the road, and we just had to sit there in the minivan for twenty minutes while they took their sweet time. \n\nMy husband, Dave, he kept trying to take pictures with this clunky old digital camera we had. It took a floppy disk, can you believe that? You could fit maybe ten pictures on one disk. Now everybody just uses their phones. The phones these days take better pictures than the professional cameras used to. My niece, Sarah, she takes all her wedding photography on her phone. She does these beautiful outdoor weddings. She had one last month at this vineyard up north. It rained, unfortunately, but they had these big white tents...\n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the zoo. Yeah, no, even in a cage at a pet store, if I saw a mouse, I'd have that same panic. My heart would go crazy and I'd just need to run.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be any different if you saw a mouse compared to seeing a rat?\n\nPatient: No, it wouldn't make a difference at all. A mouse, a rat... it's the exact same panic for me. Either one of them just sets me off completely. \n\nMy sister, though, she thinks mice are actually cute. Can you imagine? She used to read those Beatrix Potter books to her kids—you know, the ones with the little mice in the bonnets and aprons? *The Tale of Two Bad Mice*, I think one was called. They had these gorgeous illustrations. My sister always loved watercolor paintings. She actually took a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago. She painted this lovely picture of a lighthouse for my mother's birthday. \n\nMy mother loved lighthouses. She collected these little ceramic ones. She had a whole curio cabinet full of them in the dining room. Every time we went on vacation, we had to find a gift shop so she could buy a new one. We went to Cape Cod once when I was a teenager, and I swear we spent more time looking at ceramic lighthouses than we did at the actual ocean. The water there was freezing anyway. We had clam chowder in bread bowls, though, which was nice. I tried making clam chowder at home once, but it just didn't taste the same. I think I didn't use enough cream, or maybe it was the potatoes... I always buy the red potatoes, but maybe I should have used Russets. Russets are better for baking, I know that. I like a baked potato with just a little bit of butter and chives. My husband puts sour cream and bacon bits on his, but that's just too heavy for me, especially before bed.\n\nBut, um, yeah. A mouse or a rat, it doesn't matter. The size doesn't change anything for me. It's the same terrible feeling either way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I'd say it's about the same everywhere, honestly. It doesn't really matter if I'm at work or at home. I clean buildings for a living, mostly those big corporate offices downtown, you know, the ones with the massive glass lobbies. We have to use a special squeegee for those windows so they don't streak. My supervisor, Gary, he is an absolute stickler about streaks. He used to manage a car wash before he came to our cleaning company, so I guess he's just used to staring at glass all day. 

He's a nice guy, though. He brought in donuts last Friday because it was his daughter's birthday. She just turned sixteen, so she's getting her driver's license. I remember when my kids were learning to drive. My oldest, he backed right into our mailbox on his very first try. Just completely flattened it. We had to go down to the hardware store and buy a new wooden post and a new metal box and everything. The guy at the hardware store was so helpful, he even told us exactly how to mix the concrete to set the post in the ground. You have to get the water ratio just right or it won't cure properly. 

I guess it's a lot like baking, really. If you put too much flour in a cake, it gets all dry and crumbly. I made a lemon pound cake for Easter last year that turned out a little too dry, but I whipped up a nice sugar glaze for it, so nobody really complained. The secret to a good glaze is just a tiny splash of vanilla extract along with the lemon juice. It just cuts the tartness perfectly. I buy the real vanilla, too, not that imitation stuff. It costs a little more, but it's worth it...

But, um, anyway... no, it's not worse at work. It's just as bad everywhere. If I see one of those things, I panic, no matter what building I'm in.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well... I'm a building cleaner, and I'm here because... well, it's the rats. Or, just the thought of seeing a rat, really. It's gotten so bad that I can't even do parts of my job anymore, like going into the basement or the trash rooms. I mean, I know in my head it's probably just an old building and it's not really dangerous, but I just can't do it. \n\nSpeaking of the building, though, it's actually historic. They built it in the 1920s, I think? The lobby has this gorgeous original tile work. I always tell my sister, Mary—she works at a bakery down the street, makes the absolute best sourdough—I tell her they just don't make floors like that anymore. You have to use a special wax on them or it completely ruins the finish, and my supervisor never orders the right one, so I'm always having to buff it twice. It's exhausting. But anyway, what was I... oh, right. The trash rooms. I just can't go in them anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think about seeing a rat, do you ever feel like you're going to panic?\n\nPatient: Oh, panic is exactly the word for it. If I even just hear a little scratching sound near a wall, or if I see a shadow move by one of those old pipes, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake so bad, and I just feel like I have to get out of there before I can even think straight. \n\nI actually dropped my whole keyring last week because my hands were shaking so much. Which is a nightmare, because there are like forty keys on that ring. My supervisor was supposed to switch us to those electronic keycards months ago. They have them in the building next door—my friend Sarah works over there, and she just swipes her badge and the door opens. So much easier. She's got arthritis in her hands, you know, so turning those heavy deadbolts was getting really hard for her. Her doctor told her to try eating more turmeric for the inflammation, but I don't know if that actually works. Do you think spices actually do anything for joints? Because my knees have been acting up lately when I mop the main hall, and I was thinking about trying it... but I don't really like the taste of curry, so I'm not sure how I'd cook with it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the thought of rats that causes you to panic, or are there other animals or situations that trigger a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats. Or mice, I guess, anything like that. Spiders don't bother me, heights are fine... it's just the rodents. I mean, my neighbor has a pet snake and I don't mind that at all. Actually, he was telling me the other day that snakes make great pets because they're so quiet. Not like dogs. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. The judge was wearing this really bright yellow hat, though, so maybe that's what set him off. I read somewhere that dogs can't see all colors, but maybe yellow is one of the ones they can see? I don't know. But yeah, no, it's just the rats that make me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear rats or mice when you're not at work, like when you're at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. \n\nSpeaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding rats the main reason you're avoiding certain areas, like the basement or trash rooms, at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. \n\nSpeaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? They chew through walls and pipes and... oh, speaking of pipes, the plumbing in my sister's house is an absolute nightmare. She lives over in the next county, in this old Victorian house she bought for a steal. It's beautiful, all that original woodwork, but the pipes are from, like, the 1920s. \n\nShe called a plumber last week—his name was Frank, I think. Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats or mice affect your social life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if it affects my social life, honestly. I mean, I don't go out all that much anyway. I usually just go to work and come home. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. She lives in this cute little duplex over by the park. \n\nIt's a nice park, actually, they just put in new swings for the kids. The old ones were getting so rusty, I was always worried some poor child was going to get tetanus. Do they even give tetanus shots anymore? I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...\n\nBut, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that it's really just the rats and mice that cause you to panic, but I want to clarify: would you say that your fear extends to other rodents, like squirrels or hamsters, as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats and mice. I mean, squirrels are fine. They just stay up in the trees, mostly. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she actually feeds the squirrels in her backyard. She buys these huge bags of peanuts—in the shell, you know, the unsalted kind. She leaves them out on this little wooden picnic table she bought specifically for the squirrels. It's so tiny and cute. \n\nShe got it at this craft fair they hold every autumn down by the river. They have all sorts of things there—handmade soaps, knitted scarves, those birdhouses painted to look like little cottages. I bought a jar of homemade apple butter there last year. It was delicious on toast, but I went back this year and the lady who makes it wasn't there. I asked around and someone said she moved to Florida to be closer to her grandkids. I guess a lot of people do that when they retire. Florida always seems so humid to me, though. My brother went to Orlando once for a conference and he said his glasses fogged up every time he stepped outside. He works in logistics, coordinating shipping routes or something like that. It sounds complicated, all those trucks and schedules...\n\nBut, uh, no, squirrels and hamsters don't bother me at all. It's just the rats and mice that make me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would react the same way to seeing a rat or mouse in a completely different context, like at a zoo or in a pet store?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I think I'd react the exact same way. It doesn't matter where it is. If I see one, or even if I just hear scratching near a wall or see something move by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away. My heart just starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight. \n\nIt's like that fight or flight thing they talk about on those nature documentaries. I watch a lot of PBS. They have this one show on Sunday nights about the Serengeti. The lions are incredible, the way they coordinate their hunting. And the wildebeests, there are just thousands of them moving across the plains. It reminds me of when my husband and I took the kids to Yellowstone years ago. We saw a herd of bison crossing the road, and we just had to sit there in the minivan for twenty minutes while they took their sweet time. \n\nMy husband, Dave, he kept trying to take pictures with this clunky old digital camera we had. It took a floppy disk, can you believe that? You could fit maybe ten pictures on one disk. Now everybody just uses their phones. The phones these days take better pictures than the professional cameras used to. My niece, Sarah, she takes all her wedding photography on her phone. She does these beautiful outdoor weddings. She had one last month at this vineyard up north. It rained, unfortunately, but they had these big white tents...\n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the zoo. Yeah, no, even in a cage at a pet store, if I saw a mouse, I'd have that same panic. My heart would go crazy and I'd just need to run.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be any different if you saw a mouse compared to seeing a rat?\n\nPatient: No, it wouldn't make a difference at all. A mouse, a rat... it's the exact same panic for me. Either one of them just sets me off completely. \n\nMy sister, though, she thinks mice are actually cute. Can you imagine? She used to read those Beatrix Potter books to her kids—you know, the ones with the little mice in the bonnets and aprons? *The Tale of Two Bad Mice*, I think one was called. They had these gorgeous illustrations. My sister always loved watercolor paintings. She actually took a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago. She painted this lovely picture of a lighthouse for my mother's birthday. \n\nMy mother loved lighthouses. She collected these little ceramic ones. She had a whole curio cabinet full of them in the dining room. Every time we went on vacation, we had to find a gift shop so she could buy a new one. We went to Cape Cod once when I was a teenager, and I swear we spent more time looking at ceramic lighthouses than we did at the actual ocean. The water there was freezing anyway. We had clam chowder in bread bowls, though, which was nice. I tried making clam chowder at home once, but it just didn't taste the same. I think I didn't use enough cream, or maybe it was the potatoes... I always buy the red potatoes, but maybe I should have used Russets. Russets are better for baking, I know that. I like a baked potato with just a little bit of butter and chives. My husband puts sour cream and bacon bits on his, but that's just too heavy for me, especially before bed.\n\nBut, um, yeah. A mouse or a rat, it doesn't matter. The size doesn't change anything for me. It's the same terrible feeling either way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of rats and mice is worse when you're at work or is it about the same everywhere?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it's about the same everywhere, honestly. It doesn't really matter if I'm at work or at home. I clean buildings for a living, mostly those big corporate offices downtown, you know, the ones with the massive glass lobbies. We have to use a special squeegee for those windows so they don't streak. My supervisor, Gary, he is an absolute stickler about streaks. He used to manage a car wash before he came to our cleaning company, so I guess he's just used to staring at glass all day. \n\nHe's a nice guy, though. He brought in donuts last Friday because it was his daughter's birthday. She just turned sixteen, so she's getting her driver's license. I remember when my kids were learning to drive. My oldest, he backed right into our mailbox on his very first try. Just completely flattened it. We had to go down to the hardware store and buy a new wooden post and a new metal box and everything. The guy at the hardware store was so helpful, he even told us exactly how to mix the concrete to set the post in the ground. You have to get the water ratio just right or it won't cure properly. \n\nI guess it's a lot like baking, really. If you put too much flour in a cake, it gets all dry and crumbly. I made a lemon pound cake for Easter last year that turned out a little too dry, but I whipped up a nice sugar glaze for it, so nobody really complained. The secret to a good glaze is just a tiny splash of vanilla extract along with the lemon juice. It just cuts the tartness perfectly. I buy the real vanilla, too, not that imitation stuff. It costs a little more, but it's worth it...\n\nBut, um, anyway... no, it's not worse at work. It's just as bad everywhere. If I see one of those things, I panic, no matter what building I'm in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It doesn't really matter if I'm at work or at home. I clean buildings for a living, mostly those big corporate offices downtown, you know, the ones with the massive glass lobbies. We have to use a special squeegee for those windows so they don't streak. My supervisor, Gary, he is an absolute stickler about streaks. He used to manage a car wash before he came to our cleaning company, so I guess he's just used to staring at glass all day. \n\nHe's a nice guy, though. He brought in donuts last Friday because it was his daughter's birthday. She just turned sixteen, so she's getting her driver's license. I remember when my kids were learning to drive. My oldest, he backed right into our mailbox on his very first try. Just completely flattened it. We had to go down to the hardware store and buy a new wooden post and a new metal box and everything. The guy at the hardware store was so helpful, he even told us exactly how to mix the concrete to set the post in the ground. You have to get the water ratio just right or it won't cure properly. \n\nI guess it's a lot like baking, really. If you put too much flour in a cake, it gets all dry and crumbly. I made a lemon pound cake for Easter last year that turned out a little too dry, but I whipped up a nice sugar glaze for it, so nobody really complained. The secret to a good glaze is just a tiny splash of vanilla extract along with the lemon juice. It just cuts the tartness perfectly. I buy the real vanilla, too, not that imitation stuff. It costs a little more, but it's worth it...\n\nBut, um, anyway... no, it's not worse at work. It's just as bad everywhere. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, changing jobs... I don't know if the fear itself would be less intense, but it would definitely make my day-to-day life a lot easier. Right now, it's really starting to mess with my work. I've actually had to refuse or put off cleaning certain buildings because I know there's a problem there, or even just if I suspect it. And my supervisor, Gary—the one I was telling you about with the squeegees—he's starting to notice. He's been questioning whether I can be counted on for the regular routes. 

It really worries me because I was hoping to get a lead position soon, or at least some better shifts. The day shift would be so nice. Working nights means I miss all the prime-time television. I have to record everything on the DVR. My husband always forgets how to use the DVR remote, so if I don't set it up before I leave, he just ends up watching reruns of old Westerns on cable. He loves *Gunsmoke*. He's watched every single episode, I swear. He even bought a cowboy hat at a flea market a couple of years ago. He wore it while he was mowing the lawn once, and our neighbor, Mr. Henderson, asked if he was going to a rodeo. Mr. Henderson is a hoot. He grows the most amazing tomatoes in his backyard. He gave us a whole basket of them last summer. They were those big, ugly heirloom ones, but they tasted incredible. I made fresh bruschetta with them, just a little olive oil, basil, and garlic on toasted baguette slices. You really have to rub the raw garlic on the toasted bread to get the best flavor... 

But, um, yeah... if I didn't have to clean those older buildings where they might be, I wouldn't be risking my job or my chances at a promotion. So, a different job might not cure the fear, but it would stop it from ruining my career.

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well... I'm a building cleaner, and I'm here because... well, it's the rats. Or, just the thought of seeing a rat, really. It's gotten so bad that I can't even do parts of my job anymore, like going into the basement or the trash rooms. I mean, I know in my head it's probably just an old building and it's not really dangerous, but I just can't do it. \n\nSpeaking of the building, though, it's actually historic. They built it in the 1920s, I think? The lobby has this gorgeous original tile work. I always tell my sister, Mary—she works at a bakery down the street, makes the absolute best sourdough—I tell her they just don't make floors like that anymore. You have to use a special wax on them or it completely ruins the finish, and my supervisor never orders the right one, so I'm always having to buff it twice. It's exhausting. But anyway, what was I... oh, right. The trash rooms. I just can't go in them anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think about seeing a rat, do you ever feel like you're going to panic?\n\nPatient: Oh, panic is exactly the word for it. If I even just hear a little scratching sound near a wall, or if I see a shadow move by one of those old pipes, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake so bad, and I just feel like I have to get out of there before I can even think straight. \n\nI actually dropped my whole keyring last week because my hands were shaking so much. Which is a nightmare, because there are like forty keys on that ring. My supervisor was supposed to switch us to those electronic keycards months ago. They have them in the building next door—my friend Sarah works over there, and she just swipes her badge and the door opens. So much easier. She's got arthritis in her hands, you know, so turning those heavy deadbolts was getting really hard for her. Her doctor told her to try eating more turmeric for the inflammation, but I don't know if that actually works. Do you think spices actually do anything for joints? Because my knees have been acting up lately when I mop the main hall, and I was thinking about trying it... but I don't really like the taste of curry, so I'm not sure how I'd cook with it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the thought of rats that causes you to panic, or are there other animals or situations that trigger a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats. Or mice, I guess, anything like that. Spiders don't bother me, heights are fine... it's just the rodents. I mean, my neighbor has a pet snake and I don't mind that at all. Actually, he was telling me the other day that snakes make great pets because they're so quiet. Not like dogs. My sister Mary—the one at the bakery—she has this little terrier mix, and I swear that dog barks at the wind. It's so high-pitched, too. She bought one of those special collars that beeps when he barks, but I don't think he cares. He just barks over the beep. It's a shame, really, because he's a cute dog otherwise. He has this little white patch on his chest that looks exactly like a star. Mary tried to enter him in one of those local pet shows they have at the park every summer, but he got disqualified because he wouldn't stop barking at the judge. The judge was wearing this really bright yellow hat, though, so maybe that's what set him off. I read somewhere that dogs can't see all colors, but maybe yellow is one of the ones they can see? I don't know. But yeah, no, it's just the rats that make me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear rats or mice when you're not at work, like when you're at home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's everywhere, really. If I'm at home and I hear a noise in the walls, I get that same awful feeling. Even just walking past an alleyway near my apartment makes my chest tight because I'm terrified one might run out. My apartment is on the ground floor, which is nice for groceries, but terrible for worrying about... them getting in. \n\nSpeaking of groceries, though, the prices at the market down the street have gotten absolutely ridiculous. I went to buy eggs the other day, and they were almost double what they cost last month! I asked the cashier—her name is Brenda, she's worked there for years, always wears these big colorful earrings—I asked her what was going on, and she said it's something about supply chains. But honestly, I think they just know it's the only store within walking distance for most of us in the building. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he's started taking the bus all the way across town just to go to the discount supermarket. He says he saves at least twenty dollars a week doing it. He's retired, though, so he has the time. He used to be a postman, actually, and he has the most amazing collection of vintage stamps. He showed them to me once when he invited me in for tea. His wife used to collect teapots before she passed away... they had shelves and shelves of them. Some looked like little houses, some looked like animals... oh, but no rats or mice, thank goodness. I wouldn't have been able to stay in there if she had those! But yes, the fear is definitely there whether I'm at work or at home.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding rats the main reason you're avoiding certain areas, like the basement or trash rooms, at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's exactly why. I try my hardest to turn down any cleaning routes that have basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or those big alley dumpsters whenever I can. \n\nSpeaking of laundry areas, the washing machines they put in the basement of my apartment building are always breaking down. It's just ridiculous. I lost three quarters in one just last Tuesday! You try calling the 1-800 number on the sticker for a refund, and nobody ever picks up. It just rings and rings. My friend Linda said she finally got a check back in the mail for a dollar fifty, but it took her six months! Can you believe that? Six months for a dollar fifty. She bought a coffee with it to celebrate, but honestly, you can't even get a regular black coffee for that price anymore at the diner on 4th street. The waitress there, Patty, she's been working there since we were in high school together. We went to Lincoln High—go Lions! She was a cheerleader back then, actually, and she still has that same perky voice when she asks if you want cream or sugar... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trash rooms and basements at work. Yeah, if my supervisor absolutely forces me to clean one of those areas and I can't get out of it, I make sure to wedge the door wide open. I just rush through the mopping and sweeping as fast as humanly possible, and I just feel so terrified the whole entire time I'm in there. I can't even breathe right until I'm back in the main hallway.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats or mice any less intense when you're not thinking about work or being in work-related locations?\n\nPatient: No, it's really not any better. It doesn't matter if I'm at work or just sitting in my living room trying to watch TV. If I even think about one, or see a picture of one on a commercial... my heart just starts pounding. \n\nActually, speaking of commercials, have you seen that new one for the car insurance with the little animated lizard? It's on all the time now. I don't know why they think a lizard makes you want to buy car insurance. My cousin Dave used to sell insurance—life insurance, though, not car insurance. He had this little office downtown, right above the old bakery that closed last year. It's a shame about that bakery, they had the best cherry danishes. Dave would bring a whole box of them to family reunions. He had to stop eating them, though, because his doctor told him his cholesterol was too high. He went on this crazy diet where he only ate grapefruit and boiled chicken for a month. He lost ten pounds, but he was so cranky the whole time. His wife, Susan, said she was ready to move out if he didn't eat a piece of bread. They ended up taking a cruise to Alaska instead. She brought back all these pictures of glaciers... \n\nBut, um, no, the fear is exactly the same no matter where I am or what I'm thinking about. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there any situation or place where you feel completely safe from rats or mice?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think there's anywhere I feel completely safe. I mean, they can get in anywhere, right? They chew through walls and pipes and... oh, speaking of pipes, the plumbing in my sister's house is an absolute nightmare. She lives over in the next county, in this old Victorian house she bought for a steal. It's beautiful, all that original woodwork, but the pipes are from, like, the 1920s. \n\nShe called a plumber last week—his name was Frank, I think. Nice guy, wore those overalls with all the pockets. He told her she needed completely new copper piping. Do you know how much copper costs these days? It's astronomical! People are actually stealing copper wire out of streetlights just to sell it for scrap. I saw a news report about it on Channel 7. The anchor, you know, the one with the really nice hair, he was saying it's becoming an epidemic. He's been anchoring that news station since my kids were in middle school. My oldest, Michael, used to want to be a news anchor. He used to practice reading the newspaper in this deep, serious voice at the breakfast table. He ended up going into accounting instead. Much more stable, I suppose, though staring at spreadsheets all day sounds so boring. He works for a firm downtown...\n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling safe. No, I don't think there's any place where I can just turn off the worry. I'm always looking over my shoulder, checking the corners, just in case.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats or mice affect your social life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if it affects my social life, honestly. I mean, I don't go out all that much anyway. I usually just go to work and come home. Sometimes I'll go over to my friend Linda's house—she's the one who got that dollar fifty check back from the washing machine company. She lives in this cute little duplex over by the park. \n\nIt's a nice park, actually, they just put in new swings for the kids. The old ones were getting so rusty, I was always worried some poor child was going to get tetanus. Do they even give tetanus shots anymore? I remember getting one when I was a kid after I stepped on a rusty nail in my grandfather's barn. He had this huge barn out in the country, painted bright red. He used to keep horses, mostly Appaloosas. Beautiful animals, with all those spots. He had one named Freckles who loved peppermints. You'd just crinkle the wrapper and he'd come trotting right over to the fence. My grandfather used to buy those big tubs of peppermints from the wholesale club...\n\nBut, um, as for my social life, I'm not really sure what you mean. I guess I just do my normal routine, you know? I don't really notice the fear changing who I see or what I do with my friends.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that it's really just the rats and mice that cause you to panic, but I want to clarify: would you say that your fear extends to other rodents, like squirrels or hamsters, as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the rats and mice. I mean, squirrels are fine. They just stay up in the trees, mostly. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she actually feeds the squirrels in her backyard. She buys these huge bags of peanuts—in the shell, you know, the unsalted kind. She leaves them out on this little wooden picnic table she bought specifically for the squirrels. It's so tiny and cute. \n\nShe got it at this craft fair they hold every autumn down by the river. They have all sorts of things there—handmade soaps, knitted scarves, those birdhouses painted to look like little cottages. I bought a jar of homemade apple butter there last year. It was delicious on toast, but I went back this year and the lady who makes it wasn't there. I asked around and someone said she moved to Florida to be closer to her grandkids. I guess a lot of people do that when they retire. Florida always seems so humid to me, though. My brother went to Orlando once for a conference and he said his glasses fogged up every time he stepped outside. He works in logistics, coordinating shipping routes or something like that. It sounds complicated, all those trucks and schedules...\n\nBut, uh, no, squirrels and hamsters don't bother me at all. It's just the rats and mice that make me panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would react the same way to seeing a rat or mouse in a completely different context, like at a zoo or in a pet store?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I think I'd react the exact same way. It doesn't matter where it is. If I see one, or even if I just hear scratching near a wall or see something move by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away. My heart just starts pounding out of my chest, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight. \n\nIt's like that fight or flight thing they talk about on those nature documentaries. I watch a lot of PBS. They have this one show on Sunday nights about the Serengeti. The lions are incredible, the way they coordinate their hunting. And the wildebeests, there are just thousands of them moving across the plains. It reminds me of when my husband and I took the kids to Yellowstone years ago. We saw a herd of bison crossing the road, and we just had to sit there in the minivan for twenty minutes while they took their sweet time. \n\nMy husband, Dave, he kept trying to take pictures with this clunky old digital camera we had. It took a floppy disk, can you believe that? You could fit maybe ten pictures on one disk. Now everybody just uses their phones. The phones these days take better pictures than the professional cameras used to. My niece, Sarah, she takes all her wedding photography on her phone. She does these beautiful outdoor weddings. She had one last month at this vineyard up north. It rained, unfortunately, but they had these big white tents...\n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the zoo. Yeah, no, even in a cage at a pet store, if I saw a mouse, I'd have that same panic. My heart would go crazy and I'd just need to run.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be any different if you saw a mouse compared to seeing a rat?\n\nPatient: No, it wouldn't make a difference at all. A mouse, a rat... it's the exact same panic for me. Either one of them just sets me off completely. \n\nMy sister, though, she thinks mice are actually cute. Can you imagine? She used to read those Beatrix Potter books to her kids—you know, the ones with the little mice in the bonnets and aprons? *The Tale of Two Bad Mice*, I think one was called. They had these gorgeous illustrations. My sister always loved watercolor paintings. She actually took a watercolor class at the community center a few years ago. She painted this lovely picture of a lighthouse for my mother's birthday. \n\nMy mother loved lighthouses. She collected these little ceramic ones. She had a whole curio cabinet full of them in the dining room. Every time we went on vacation, we had to find a gift shop so she could buy a new one. We went to Cape Cod once when I was a teenager, and I swear we spent more time looking at ceramic lighthouses than we did at the actual ocean. The water there was freezing anyway. We had clam chowder in bread bowls, though, which was nice. I tried making clam chowder at home once, but it just didn't taste the same. I think I didn't use enough cream, or maybe it was the potatoes... I always buy the red potatoes, but maybe I should have used Russets. Russets are better for baking, I know that. I like a baked potato with just a little bit of butter and chives. My husband puts sour cream and bacon bits on his, but that's just too heavy for me, especially before bed.\n\nBut, um, yeah. A mouse or a rat, it doesn't matter. The size doesn't change anything for me. It's the same terrible feeling either way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of rats and mice is worse when you're at work or is it about the same everywhere?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it's about the same everywhere, honestly. It doesn't really matter if I'm at work or at home. I clean buildings for a living, mostly those big corporate offices downtown, you know, the ones with the massive glass lobbies. We have to use a special squeegee for those windows so they don't streak. My supervisor, Gary, he is an absolute stickler about streaks. He used to manage a car wash before he came to our cleaning company, so I guess he's just used to staring at glass all day. \n\nHe's a nice guy, though. He brought in donuts last Friday because it was his daughter's birthday. She just turned sixteen, so she's getting her driver's license. I remember when my kids were learning to drive. My oldest, he backed right into our mailbox on his very first try. Just completely flattened it. We had to go down to the hardware store and buy a new wooden post and a new metal box and everything. The guy at the hardware store was so helpful, he even told us exactly how to mix the concrete to set the post in the ground. You have to get the water ratio just right or it won't cure properly. \n\nI guess it's a lot like baking, really. If you put too much flour in a cake, it gets all dry and crumbly. I made a lemon pound cake for Easter last year that turned out a little too dry, but I whipped up a nice sugar glaze for it, so nobody really complained. The secret to a good glaze is just a tiny splash of vanilla extract along with the lemon juice. It just cuts the tartness perfectly. I buy the real vanilla, too, not that imitation stuff. It costs a little more, but it's worth it...\n\nBut, um, anyway... no, it's not worse at work. It's just as bad everywhere. If I see one of those things, I panic, no matter what building I'm in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats and mice would be less intense if you were to change jobs to something that doesn't involve cleaning or being in areas where rats and mice are more likely to be found?\n\nPatient: Well, changing jobs... I don't know if the fear itself would be less intense, but it would definitely make my day-to-day life a lot easier. Right now, it's really starting to mess with my work. I've actually had to refuse or put off cleaning certain buildings because I know there's a problem there, or even just if I suspect it. And my supervisor, Gary—the one I was telling you about with the squeegees—he's starting to notice. He's been questioning whether I can be counted on for the regular routes. \n\nIt really worries me because I was hoping to get a lead position soon, or at least some better shifts. The day shift would be so nice. Working nights means I miss all the prime-time television. I have to record everything on the DVR. My husband always forgets how to use the DVR remote, so if I don't set it up before I leave, he just ends up watching reruns of old Westerns on cable. He loves *Gunsmoke*. He's watched every single episode, I swear. He even bought a cowboy hat at a flea market a couple of years ago. He wore it while he was mowing the lawn once, and our neighbor, Mr. Henderson, asked if he was going to a rodeo. Mr. Henderson is a hoot. He grows the most amazing tomatoes in his backyard. He gave us a whole basket of them last summer. They were those big, ugly heirloom ones, but they tasted incredible. I made fresh bruschetta with them, just a little olive oil, basil, and garlic on toasted baguette slices. You really have to rub the raw garlic on the toasted bread to get the best flavor... \n\nBut, um, yeah... if I didn't have to clean those older buildings where they might be, I wouldn't be risking my job or my chances at a promotion. So, a different job might not cure the fear, but it would stop it from ruining my career.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's not something I've always had, no. It really started about two years ago. That's when we moved into this older apartment building across town. It's one of those pre-war buildings, you know, with the thick plaster walls and the radiators that clank all winter long. The radiators are actually really nice because they dry out your wet winter coats so fast. I always hang my heavy wool coat on a chair right next to it. 

I bought that coat at a second-hand shop on Main Street. It was such a steal, only twenty dollars, and it has these beautiful tortoiseshell buttons. I love finding bargains like that. My friend Carol and I used to go thrift shopping every Saturday morning. We'd get up early, grab a coffee at the diner, and just hunt for treasures all day. Carol is the one who found this amazing vintage Pyrex bowl set, the one with the primary colors. She uses them for everything now. She makes this incredible potato salad for the Fourth of July block party every year. She puts a little bit of dill pickle relish in it, which I never thought of doing, but it gives it such a nice tang. I usually just use celery and hard-boiled eggs in mine, but maybe I'll try the relish next time... 

But anyway, yeah, when we moved into that apartment building two years ago, people in the building kept talking about rats. Just constantly mentioning them in the laundry room or the hallway. And that's really when this whole terrible fear started for me.
